What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Goal_Activity\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can’t seem to stop launching projects—work plans, certification schedules, home network upgrades, messages to old contacts—and it gets so intense that I blow past normal limits until my wife or my director has to step in.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I’m here because things have kind of gotten out of hand with how much I’m trying to do. It’s not just being busy. I mean, I have a demanding job already—I manage computer systems and projects, so I’m used to juggling a lot—but lately I keep launching into these big plans one after another, like work initiatives, certification schedules, redesigning my home network, reaching out to old contacts with long messages and ideas… and I can’t seem to stop once I get going.

It starts feeling important, like if I don’t act on it right now I’m going to lose the momentum or miss some opportunity. Then I blow past normal limits—staying in planning mode, sending too many messages, taking on too much—and eventually my wife has to tell me I’m going too far, or at work my director has to rein me in. That’s basically why I came in. It feels like I’m driving with the accelerator stuck down.

Yeah… sometimes. “Happy” is maybe not quite the word I’d use the whole time, but there are definitely weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m firing on all cylinders and everything is lining up and I can just keep pushing forward. I wouldn’t say it’s like clockwork, no. It’s not like every three months or on some predictable schedule that I can point to. It’s more that I’ll have stretches—some weeks—where I feel almost electric and unstoppable, like everything feels urgent and possible and I’m moving faster than everyone else around me.

And then, during those stretches, my mood can flip really quickly into irritation. Like a tiny delay, or my wife just asking me a reasonable question, can set me off. I’ll feel like she’s trying to slow me down on purpose, even if later I can tell that’s not really fair. In the moment it feels like people are blocking me when I’m finally getting traction. So… not regular intervals exactly, but these periods do seem to come in runs.

Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just being tired or having an off morning. There are stretches where I wake up and it’s like before I even open my eyes fully, I already feel this weight on me. And it can last most of the day. I’ll be sitting there knowing I need to get up, answer a message, respond to my wife asking something totally normal like, “Did you want coffee?” or “What time are you leaving?” and even that feels like too much to process. Not because she’s doing anything wrong. It’s just like there’s no room in me for anything, even basic conversation. I feel empty, kind of shut down, and I hate it because I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m being cold or difficult, but inside I’m just… flattened.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… a lot of the time it does feel like there’s a crash afterward. I don’t know if it’s always immediately after, like a clean pattern where one ends and the other begins, but I’ll go from that wired, driven feeling to these stretches where I wake up already feeling like I’ve failed somehow.

It’s hard to explain because nothing necessarily has to happen first. I’ll open my eyes and it’s just there—this heavy, flat feeling, like I’m a disappointing version of myself. And then even really normal things feel like too much. My wife might ask something simple, like what time I’m leaving or whether I want coffee, and I know it’s a normal question, but inside it feels like I don’t have enough space to answer. Like even forming a response is work.

So… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.

What I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.

So… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.

Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.

But even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.

And I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.

So I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.

Then I blow past normal limits—staying in planning mode, sending too many messages, taking on too much—and eventually my wife has to tell me I’m going too far, or at work my director has to rein me in. That’s basically why I came in. It feels like I’m driving with the accelerator stuck down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unusually happy or upbeat when you're launching into these big plans?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. “Happy” is maybe not quite the word I’d use the whole time, but there are definitely weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m firing on all cylinders and everything is lining up and I can just keep pushing forward. It’s this unstoppable feeling, like I can see the whole map in my head and I’m finally moving fast enough to match it.\n\nBut it can turn really fast. Like if my wife asks a reasonable question—“Do you need to do this tonight?” or “Can this wait?”—or if there’s some tiny delay, it feels way bigger than it should. I’ll snap or get irritated, like they’re deliberately putting brakes on me, even though later I can see that’s probably not what’s happening. In the moment it feels like everyone is trying to slow me down on purpose, and I get this surge of frustration, like, “Why are you blocking me when I’m finally getting somewhere?”\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling electric and unstoppable happen at fairly regular intervals?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s like clockwork, no. It’s not like every three months or on some predictable schedule that I can point to. It’s more that I’ll have stretches—some weeks—where I feel almost electric and unstoppable, like everything feels urgent and possible and I’m moving faster than everyone else around me.\n\nAnd then, during those stretches, my mood can flip really quickly into irritation. Like a tiny delay, or my wife just asking me a reasonable question, can set me off. I’ll feel like she’s trying to slow me down on purpose, even if later I can tell that’s not really fair. In the moment it feels like people are blocking me when I’m finally getting traction. So… not regular intervals exactly, but these periods do seem to come in runs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just being tired or having an off morning. There are stretches where I wake up and it’s like before I even open my eyes fully, I already feel this weight on me. Like I’m a failed version of myself, if that makes sense. Not one specific failure, necessarily—more like my whole self feels… disappointing. Heavy and flat.\n\nAnd it can last most of the day. I’ll be sitting there knowing I need to get up, answer a message, respond to my wife asking something totally normal like, “Did you want coffee?” or “What time are you leaving?” and even that feels like too much to process. Not because she’s doing anything wrong. It’s just like there’s no room in me for anything, even basic conversation. I feel empty, kind of shut down, and I hate it because I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m being cold or difficult, but inside I’m just… flattened.\n\nDoctor: Do your stretches of feeling electric and unstoppable usually end with you feeling sad or empty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of the time it does feel like there’s a crash afterward. I don’t know if it’s always immediately after, like a clean pattern where one ends and the other begins, but I’ll go from that wired, driven feeling to these stretches where I wake up already feeling like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because nothing necessarily has to happen first. I’ll open my eyes and it’s just there—this heavy, flat feeling, like I’m a disappointing version of myself. And then even really normal things feel like too much. My wife might ask something simple, like what time I’m leaving or whether I want coffee, and I know it’s a normal question, but inside it feels like I don’t have enough space to answer. Like even forming a response is work.\n\nSo… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Does the sad or empty feeling usually start right after one of these electric periods ends?\n\nPatient: Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.\n\nAnd I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.\n\nSo I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F30.0", "F31.0", "F25.0"], "note": "Added F25.0 due to the patient's reports of hearing brief comments when no one is there, which could indicate psychotic symptoms", "is_final": false}

Like even forming a response is work.\n\nSo… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Does the sad or empty feeling usually start right after one of these electric periods ends?\n\nPatient: Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.\n\nAnd I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.\n\nSo I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you hear these brief comments when no one is there often?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"I’ll hear my name or short comments like ‘check the logs’ or ‘they know’ in the server-room hum or from my home office when nobody else is there.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… often enough that I notice it, I guess. Not constantly, like it isn’t a full conversation happening all day, but it happens more than I’m comfortable admitting.

It’s usually brief—like I’ll hear my name, just my name, in this way that makes me stop what I’m doing. Or it’ll be a short comment, almost like it’s tucked inside background noise. At work, especially around the server-room hum, I’ll think I hear something like, “check the logs,” and I’ll actually pause because that sounds like something relevant to my job, so part of me is like, okay, did someone say that or did I just… hear it in the noise?

At home it happens too, in my office when nobody else is there. Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. And then I’m sitting there trying to figure out if it came from outside, or the computer speakers, or if my wife said something from another room, but there isn’t anyone there. It’s not usually loud and dramatic. It’s more like a comment slipping through the hum of things. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.

Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. And then I’m sitting there trying to figure out if it came from outside, or the computer speakers, or if my wife said something from another room, but there isn’t anyone there. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you hear these comments when you're feeling electric and unstoppable, when you're feeling sad or empty, or at any time regardless of your mood?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not only when I’m electric, no. That would almost make more sense to me, honestly, like if it only happened when I was sped up and not sleeping much or feeling unstoppable. And it’s not only when I’m really low either.

There have been these stretches where I’m… I don’t know, calmer? Flat maybe. Not especially up, not in that awful heavy depression either. I’m still going to work, answering emails, doing the regular routine, so from the outside I probably seem more normal. But even then, sometimes for a few weeks at a time, I’ll still get that feeling that things are being signaled to me.

Like I mentioned with Teams statuses—the little green, yellow, red indicators, or when someone changes availability—it can start feeling like coded warnings. And during those same calmer stretches, I’ll still hear the brief comments too, even when no one is there. Not all day, not like a constant voice, but enough that I stop and check.

So I guess the answer is… it can happen regardless of my mood. It may feel more intense when I’m already revved up or really down, but it doesn’t disappear just because I’m not in one of those states. That’s part of what scares me a little, because I can’t just explain it away as “oh, that was only when I was electric” or “only when I was depressed.”

Like if my wife asks a reasonable question—“Do you need to do this tonight?” or “Can this wait?”—or if there’s some tiny delay, it feels way bigger than it should. I’ll snap or get irritated, like they’re deliberately putting brakes on me, even though later I can see that’s probably not what’s happening. In the moment it feels like everyone is trying to slow me down on purpose, and I get this surge of frustration, like, “Why are you blocking me when I’m finally getting somewhere?”\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling electric and unstoppable happen at fairly regular intervals?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s like clockwork, no. It’s not like every three months or on some predictable schedule that I can point to. It’s more that I’ll have stretches—some weeks—where I feel almost electric and unstoppable, like everything feels urgent and possible and I’m moving faster than everyone else around me.\n\nAnd then, during those stretches, my mood can flip really quickly into irritation. Like a tiny delay, or my wife just asking me a reasonable question, can set me off. I’ll feel like she’s trying to slow me down on purpose, even if later I can tell that’s not really fair. In the moment it feels like people are blocking me when I’m finally getting traction. So… not regular intervals exactly, but these periods do seem to come in runs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just being tired or having an off morning. There are stretches where I wake up and it’s like before I even open my eyes fully, I already feel this weight on me. Like I’m a failed version of myself, if that makes sense. Not one specific failure, necessarily—more like my whole self feels… disappointing. Heavy and flat.\n\nAnd it can last most of the day. I’ll be sitting there knowing I need to get up, answer a message, respond to my wife asking something totally normal like, “Did you want coffee?” or “What time are you leaving?” and even that feels like too much to process. Not because she’s doing anything wrong. It’s just like there’s no room in me for anything, even basic conversation. I feel empty, kind of shut down, and I hate it because I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m being cold or difficult, but inside I’m just… flattened.\n\nDoctor: Do your stretches of feeling electric and unstoppable usually end with you feeling sad or empty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of the time it does feel like there’s a crash afterward. I don’t know if it’s always immediately after, like a clean pattern where one ends and the other begins, but I’ll go from that wired, driven feeling to these stretches where I wake up already feeling like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because nothing necessarily has to happen first. I’ll open my eyes and it’s just there—this heavy, flat feeling, like I’m a disappointing version of myself. And then even really normal things feel like too much. My wife might ask something simple, like what time I’m leaving or whether I want coffee, and I know it’s a normal question, but inside it feels like I don’t have enough space to answer. Like even forming a response is work.\n\nSo… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Does the sad or empty feeling usually start right after one of these electric periods ends?\n\nPatient: Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.\n\nAnd I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.\n\nSo I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these brief comments when no one is there often?\n\nPatient: Yeah… often enough that I notice it, I guess. Not constantly, like it isn’t a full conversation happening all day, but it happens more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nIt’s usually brief—like I’ll hear my name, just my name, in this way that makes me stop what I’m doing. Or it’ll be a short comment, almost like it’s tucked inside background noise. At work, especially around the server-room hum, I’ll think I hear something like, “check the logs,” and I’ll actually pause because that sounds like something relevant to my job, so part of me is like, okay, did someone say that or did I just… hear it in the noise?\n\nAt home it happens too, in my office when nobody else is there. Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. And then I’m sitting there trying to figure out if it came from outside, or the computer speakers, or if my wife said something from another room, but there isn’t anyone there. It’s not usually loud and dramatic. It’s more like a comment slipping through the hum of things. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these comments when you're feeling electric and unstoppable, when you're feeling sad or empty, or at any time regardless of your mood?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m electric, no. That would almost make more sense to me, honestly, like if it only happened when I was sped up and not sleeping much or feeling unstoppable. And it’s not only when I’m really low either.\n\nThere have been these stretches where I’m… I don’t know, calmer? Flat maybe. Not especially up, not in that awful heavy depression either. I’m still going to work, answering emails, doing the regular routine, so from the outside I probably seem more normal. But even then, sometimes for a few weeks at a time, I’ll still get that feeling that things are being signaled to me.\n\nLike I mentioned with Teams statuses—the little green, yellow, red indicators, or when someone changes availability—it can start feeling like coded warnings. And during those same calmer stretches, I’ll still hear the brief comments too, even when no one is there. Not all day, not like a constant voice, but enough that I stop and check.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… it can happen regardless of my mood. It may feel more intense when I’m already revved up or really down, but it doesn’t disappear just because I’m not in one of those states. That’s part of what scares me a little, because I can’t just explain it away as “oh, that was only when I was electric” or “only when I was depressed.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It seems like background hum is a big trigger, or maybe not a trigger exactly, but where it slips in the most. Like in the server room at work, with that constant fan noise and equipment noise, I’ll suddenly hear my name or something short, like “check the logs,” and it feels just clear enough that I stop and listen. Since that phrase actually relates to my job, it can take me a second to separate whether it was real or just… in the hum.

At home it can happen in my office too, especially if it’s quiet but there’s still some kind of computer noise or house noise. I’ll hear something like “they know,” and I’ll look around or pause whatever I’m doing, because it feels like it came from somewhere nearby even though nobody else is there.

So, yeah, it’s not usually out of total silence. It’s more like it gets shaped out of background noise, if that makes sense. The server-room hum is probably the clearest example.

That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. heard brief comments when no one was there.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I was put on leave after the migration failed, and at home my wife has had to step in around spending, late-night work, and conversations that turn into arguments. I’ve also pulled away from church music, climbing, and mentoring because I can’t reliably show up or follow through.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 2 years and 5 months, since I took on the major system migration role at work.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 6 months\n  manifestation: The coded-message beliefs and hearing brief comments have been happening for about a year and a half.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The unusually driven, low-sleep, overconfident stretches have been part of my life for roughly 1 year and 8 months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: The low, withdrawn periods have added up to about 7 months during this whole course.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you hear these brief comments when no one is there often?\n\nPatient: Yeah… often enough that I notice it, I guess. Not constantly, like it isn’t a full conversation happening all day, but it happens more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nIt’s usually brief—like I’ll hear my name, just my name, in this way that makes me stop what I’m doing. Or it’ll be a short comment, almost like it’s tucked inside background noise. At work, especially around the server-room hum, I’ll think I hear something like, “check the logs,” and I’ll actually pause because that sounds like something relevant to my job, so part of me is like, okay, did someone say that or did I just… hear it in the noise?\n\nAt home it happens too, in my office when nobody else is there. Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. Like in the server room at work, with that constant fan noise and equipment noise, I’ll suddenly hear my name or something short, like “check the logs,” and it feels just clear enough that I stop and listen. I’ll hear something like “they know,” and I’ll look around or pause whatever I’m doing, because it feels like it came from somewhere nearby even though nobody else is there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not usually out of total silence. It’s more like it gets shaped out of background noise, if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Usually they’re more like short phrases than full instructions. Like I’ll hear my name, or something like “they know,” and there isn’t really an action attached to that—it just makes me stop and feel like I’m supposed to understand something.

“Check the logs” is the one that feels closest to telling me to do something, because that actually is an action, and it fits my job so well that I’ll sometimes pause and think, okay, should I check them? Did someone say that because there’s a problem? But it’s not like a whole voice giving me a list of commands or telling me step-by-step what to do. It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.

That’s basically why I came in. It feels like I’m driving with the accelerator stuck down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unusually happy or upbeat when you're launching into these big plans?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. “Happy” is maybe not quite the word I’d use the whole time, but there are definitely weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m firing on all cylinders and everything is lining up and I can just keep pushing forward. It’s this unstoppable feeling, like I can see the whole map in my head and I’m finally moving fast enough to match it.\n\nBut it can turn really fast. Like if my wife asks a reasonable question—“Do you need to do this tonight?” or “Can this wait?”—or if there’s some tiny delay, it feels way bigger than it should. I’ll snap or get irritated, like they’re deliberately putting brakes on me, even though later I can see that’s probably not what’s happening. In the moment it feels like everyone is trying to slow me down on purpose, and I get this surge of frustration, like, “Why are you blocking me when I’m finally getting somewhere?”\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling electric and unstoppable happen at fairly regular intervals?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s like clockwork, no. It’s not like every three months or on some predictable schedule that I can point to. It’s more that I’ll have stretches—some weeks—where I feel almost electric and unstoppable, like everything feels urgent and possible and I’m moving faster than everyone else around me.\n\nAnd then, during those stretches, my mood can flip really quickly into irritation. Like a tiny delay, or my wife just asking me a reasonable question, can set me off. I’ll feel like she’s trying to slow me down on purpose, even if later I can tell that’s not really fair. In the moment it feels like people are blocking me when I’m finally getting traction. So… not regular intervals exactly, but these periods do seem to come in runs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just being tired or having an off morning. There are stretches where I wake up and it’s like before I even open my eyes fully, I already feel this weight on me. Like I’m a failed version of myself, if that makes sense. Not one specific failure, necessarily—more like my whole self feels… disappointing. Heavy and flat.\n\nAnd it can last most of the day. I’ll be sitting there knowing I need to get up, answer a message, respond to my wife asking something totally normal like, “Did you want coffee?” or “What time are you leaving?” and even that feels like too much to process. Not because she’s doing anything wrong. It’s just like there’s no room in me for anything, even basic conversation. I feel empty, kind of shut down, and I hate it because I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m being cold or difficult, but inside I’m just… flattened.\n\nDoctor: Do your stretches of feeling electric and unstoppable usually end with you feeling sad or empty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of the time it does feel like there’s a crash afterward. I don’t know if it’s always immediately after, like a clean pattern where one ends and the other begins, but I’ll go from that wired, driven feeling to these stretches where I wake up already feeling like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because nothing necessarily has to happen first. I’ll open my eyes and it’s just there—this heavy, flat feeling, like I’m a disappointing version of myself. And then even really normal things feel like too much. My wife might ask something simple, like what time I’m leaving or whether I want coffee, and I know it’s a normal question, but inside it feels like I don’t have enough space to answer. Like even forming a response is work.\n\nSo… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Does the sad or empty feeling usually start right after one of these electric periods ends?\n\nPatient: Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.\n\nAnd I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.\n\nSo I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these brief comments when no one is there often?\n\nPatient: Yeah… often enough that I notice it, I guess. Not constantly, like it isn’t a full conversation happening all day, but it happens more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nIt’s usually brief—like I’ll hear my name, just my name, in this way that makes me stop what I’m doing. Or it’ll be a short comment, almost like it’s tucked inside background noise. At work, especially around the server-room hum, I’ll think I hear something like, “check the logs,” and I’ll actually pause because that sounds like something relevant to my job, so part of me is like, okay, did someone say that or did I just… hear it in the noise?\n\nAt home it happens too, in my office when nobody else is there. Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. And then I’m sitting there trying to figure out if it came from outside, or the computer speakers, or if my wife said something from another room, but there isn’t anyone there. It’s not usually loud and dramatic. It’s more like a comment slipping through the hum of things. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these comments when you're feeling electric and unstoppable, when you're feeling sad or empty, or at any time regardless of your mood?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m electric, no. That would almost make more sense to me, honestly, like if it only happened when I was sped up and not sleeping much or feeling unstoppable. And it’s not only when I’m really low either.\n\nThere have been these stretches where I’m… I don’t know, calmer? Flat maybe. Not especially up, not in that awful heavy depression either. I’m still going to work, answering emails, doing the regular routine, so from the outside I probably seem more normal. But even then, sometimes for a few weeks at a time, I’ll still get that feeling that things are being signaled to me.\n\nLike I mentioned with Teams statuses—the little green, yellow, red indicators, or when someone changes availability—it can start feeling like coded warnings. And during those same calmer stretches, I’ll still hear the brief comments too, even when no one is there. Not all day, not like a constant voice, but enough that I stop and check.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… it can happen regardless of my mood. It may feel more intense when I’m already revved up or really down, but it doesn’t disappear just because I’m not in one of those states. That’s part of what scares me a little, because I can’t just explain it away as “oh, that was only when I was electric” or “only when I was depressed.”\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear when no one is there seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It seems like background hum is a big trigger, or maybe not a trigger exactly, but where it slips in the most. Like in the server room at work, with that constant fan noise and equipment noise, I’ll suddenly hear my name or something short, like “check the logs,” and it feels just clear enough that I stop and listen. Since that phrase actually relates to my job, it can take me a second to separate whether it was real or just… in the hum.\n\nAt home it can happen in my office too, especially if it’s quiet but there’s still some kind of computer noise or house noise. I’ll hear something like “they know,” and I’ll look around or pause whatever I’m doing, because it feels like it came from somewhere nearby even though nobody else is there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not usually out of total silence. It’s more like it gets shaped out of background noise, if that makes sense. The server-room hum is probably the clearest example.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these brief comments, are they usually telling you to do something or are they just phrases without any specific action?\n\nPatient: Usually they’re more like short phrases than full instructions. Like I’ll hear my name, or something like “they know,” and there isn’t really an action attached to that—it just makes me stop and feel like I’m supposed to understand something.\n\n“Check the logs” is the one that feels closest to telling me to do something, because that actually is an action, and it fits my job so well that I’ll sometimes pause and think, okay, should I check them? Did someone say that because there’s a problem? But it’s not like a whole voice giving me a list of commands or telling me step-by-step what to do. It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s a mix, I think, but the ones I’ve been trying to describe are not just thoughts, at least they don’t feel like my normal thoughts. Like, I know what it feels like when a thought pops into my head—like remembering I forgot to send an email or suddenly thinking about a meeting. That feels internal, like it’s in my own stream.

These are different. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.

Sometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? But in the moment, it lands more like I heard it. It’s brief, not a whole conversation, but it makes me stop what I’m doing and listen for a second.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because things have kind of gotten out of hand with how much I’m trying to do. It’s not just being busy. I mean, I have a demanding job already—I manage computer systems and projects, so I’m used to juggling a lot—but lately I keep launching into these big plans one after another, like work initiatives, certification schedules, redesigning my home network, reaching out to old contacts with long messages and ideas… and I can’t seem to stop once I get going.\n\nIt starts feeling important, like if I don’t act on it right now I’m going to lose the momentum or miss some opportunity. Then I blow past normal limits—staying in planning mode, sending too many messages, taking on too much—and eventually my wife has to tell me I’m going too far, or at work my director has to rein me in. That’s basically why I came in. It feels like I’m driving with the accelerator stuck down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unusually happy or upbeat when you're launching into these big plans?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. “Happy” is maybe not quite the word I’d use the whole time, but there are definitely weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m firing on all cylinders and everything is lining up and I can just keep pushing forward. It’s this unstoppable feeling, like I can see the whole map in my head and I’m finally moving fast enough to match it.\n\nBut it can turn really fast. Like if my wife asks a reasonable question—“Do you need to do this tonight?” or “Can this wait?”—or if there’s some tiny delay, it feels way bigger than it should. I’ll snap or get irritated, like they’re deliberately putting brakes on me, even though later I can see that’s probably not what’s happening. In the moment it feels like everyone is trying to slow me down on purpose, and I get this surge of frustration, like, “Why are you blocking me when I’m finally getting somewhere?”\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling electric and unstoppable happen at fairly regular intervals?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s like clockwork, no. It’s not like every three months or on some predictable schedule that I can point to. It’s more that I’ll have stretches—some weeks—where I feel almost electric and unstoppable, like everything feels urgent and possible and I’m moving faster than everyone else around me.\n\nAnd then, during those stretches, my mood can flip really quickly into irritation. Like a tiny delay, or my wife just asking me a reasonable question, can set me off. I’ll feel like she’s trying to slow me down on purpose, even if later I can tell that’s not really fair. In the moment it feels like people are blocking me when I’m finally getting traction. So… not regular intervals exactly, but these periods do seem to come in runs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just being tired or having an off morning. There are stretches where I wake up and it’s like before I even open my eyes fully, I already feel this weight on me. Like I’m a failed version of myself, if that makes sense. Not one specific failure, necessarily—more like my whole self feels… disappointing. Heavy and flat.\n\nAnd it can last most of the day. I’ll be sitting there knowing I need to get up, answer a message, respond to my wife asking something totally normal like, “Did you want coffee?” or “What time are you leaving?” and even that feels like too much to process. Not because she’s doing anything wrong. It’s just like there’s no room in me for anything, even basic conversation. I feel empty, kind of shut down, and I hate it because I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m being cold or difficult, but inside I’m just… flattened.\n\nDoctor: Do your stretches of feeling electric and unstoppable usually end with you feeling sad or empty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of the time it does feel like there’s a crash afterward. I don’t know if it’s always immediately after, like a clean pattern where one ends and the other begins, but I’ll go from that wired, driven feeling to these stretches where I wake up already feeling like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because nothing necessarily has to happen first. I’ll open my eyes and it’s just there—this heavy, flat feeling, like I’m a disappointing version of myself. And then even really normal things feel like too much. My wife might ask something simple, like what time I’m leaving or whether I want coffee, and I know it’s a normal question, but inside it feels like I don’t have enough space to answer. Like even forming a response is work.\n\nSo… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Does the sad or empty feeling usually start right after one of these electric periods ends?\n\nPatient: Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.\n\nAnd I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.\n\nSo I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these brief comments when no one is there often?\n\nPatient: Yeah… often enough that I notice it, I guess. Not constantly, like it isn’t a full conversation happening all day, but it happens more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nIt’s usually brief—like I’ll hear my name, just my name, in this way that makes me stop what I’m doing. Or it’ll be a short comment, almost like it’s tucked inside background noise. At work, especially around the server-room hum, I’ll think I hear something like, “check the logs,” and I’ll actually pause because that sounds like something relevant to my job, so part of me is like, okay, did someone say that or did I just… hear it in the noise?\n\nAt home it happens too, in my office when nobody else is there. Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. And then I’m sitting there trying to figure out if it came from outside, or the computer speakers, or if my wife said something from another room, but there isn’t anyone there. It’s not usually loud and dramatic. It’s more like a comment slipping through the hum of things. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these comments when you're feeling electric and unstoppable, when you're feeling sad or empty, or at any time regardless of your mood?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m electric, no. That would almost make more sense to me, honestly, like if it only happened when I was sped up and not sleeping much or feeling unstoppable. And it’s not only when I’m really low either.\n\nThere have been these stretches where I’m… I don’t know, calmer? Flat maybe. Not especially up, not in that awful heavy depression either. I’m still going to work, answering emails, doing the regular routine, so from the outside I probably seem more normal. But even then, sometimes for a few weeks at a time, I’ll still get that feeling that things are being signaled to me.\n\nLike I mentioned with Teams statuses—the little green, yellow, red indicators, or when someone changes availability—it can start feeling like coded warnings. And during those same calmer stretches, I’ll still hear the brief comments too, even when no one is there. Not all day, not like a constant voice, but enough that I stop and check.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… it can happen regardless of my mood. It may feel more intense when I’m already revved up or really down, but it doesn’t disappear just because I’m not in one of those states. That’s part of what scares me a little, because I can’t just explain it away as “oh, that was only when I was electric” or “only when I was depressed.”\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear when no one is there seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It seems like background hum is a big trigger, or maybe not a trigger exactly, but where it slips in the most. Like in the server room at work, with that constant fan noise and equipment noise, I’ll suddenly hear my name or something short, like “check the logs,” and it feels just clear enough that I stop and listen. Since that phrase actually relates to my job, it can take me a second to separate whether it was real or just… in the hum.\n\nAt home it can happen in my office too, especially if it’s quiet but there’s still some kind of computer noise or house noise. I’ll hear something like “they know,” and I’ll look around or pause whatever I’m doing, because it feels like it came from somewhere nearby even though nobody else is there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not usually out of total silence. It’s more like it gets shaped out of background noise, if that makes sense. The server-room hum is probably the clearest example.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these brief comments, are they usually telling you to do something or are they just phrases without any specific action?\n\nPatient: Usually they’re more like short phrases than full instructions. Like I’ll hear my name, or something like “they know,” and there isn’t really an action attached to that—it just makes me stop and feel like I’m supposed to understand something.\n\n“Check the logs” is the one that feels closest to telling me to do something, because that actually is an action, and it fits my job so well that I’ll sometimes pause and think, okay, should I check them? Did someone say that because there’s a problem? But it’s not like a whole voice giving me a list of commands or telling me step-by-step what to do. It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you always hear the brief comments, or are they sometimes more like thoughts that pop into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s a mix, I think, but the ones I’ve been trying to describe are not just thoughts, at least they don’t feel like my normal thoughts. Like, I know what it feels like when a thought pops into my head—like remembering I forgot to send an email or suddenly thinking about a meeting. That feels internal, like it’s in my own stream.\n\nThese are different. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? ---------- turn 13 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type). It’s only something I hear, as far as I can tell. I don’t see anything with it, like no shapes or people or anything moving around that shouldn’t be there.

It’s more that the sound in the room starts to feel like it has words in it. Like in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll hear my name or something short like “check the logs,” and I’ll stop because for a second it seems like someone nearby said it. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you always hear the brief comments, or are they sometimes more like thoughts that pop into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s a mix, I think, but the ones I’ve been trying to describe are not just thoughts, at least they don’t feel like my normal thoughts. Like, I know what it feels like when a thought pops into my head—like remembering I forgot to send an email or suddenly thinking about a meeting. That feels internal, like it’s in my own stream.\n\nThese are different. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? But in the moment, it lands more like I heard it. It’s brief, not a whole conversation, but it makes me stop what I’m doing and listen for a second.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these brief comments, do you ever see anything unusual or is it only something you hear?\n\nPatient: It’s only something I hear, as far as I can tell. I don’t see anything with it, like no shapes or people or anything moving around that shouldn’t be there.\n\nIt’s more that the sound in the room starts to feel like it has words in it. Like in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll hear my name or something short like “check the logs,” and I’ll stop because for a second it seems like someone nearby said it. Or at home in my office, when I’m alone, I might hear something like “they know.” But I’m not seeing anyone there when it happens. I’ll look around sometimes, just automatically, but there’s nothing visual that goes along with it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo the brief comments you hear happen more often when you're in one of your electric or sad periods?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"During my most driven stretches, when I was barely sleeping and pushing huge plans, I was also hearing comments in the server noise and reading hidden threats into work messages.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, they seem to happen more in the “electric” periods, if that’s what you mean by the driven ones. Like when I’m barely sleeping and I’m really keyed up, pushing these huge plans at work or in my head, the comments in the server noise get more noticeable.

It’s not just that I’m tired and mishearing things—although maybe that’s part of it—but during those stretches I’ll hear something like “check the logs” or my name in the equipment hum, and it feels more loaded, like it connects to whatever I’m working on. I also start reading more into work messages then, like emails or chat messages feel like they have hidden threats or warnings in them, even if later I can sometimes see maybe they were just normal messages.

When I’m sad, I don’t know… I’m more shut down, and I’m not usually pushing all those big plans. I might still be anxious or stuck in my head, but the brief comments seem more tied to those driven, barely-sleeping periods than the sad ones.
