[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF30.0, F31.0, F25.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because things have kind of gotten out of hand with how much I’m trying to do. It’s not just being busy. I mean, I have a demanding job already—I manage computer systems and projects, so I’m used to juggling a lot—but lately I keep launching into these big plans one after another, like work initiatives, certification schedules, redesigning my home network, reaching out to old contacts with long messages and ideas… and I can’t seem to stop once I get going.\n\nIt starts feeling important, like if I don’t act on it right now I’m going to lose the momentum or miss some opportunity. Then I blow past normal limits—staying in planning mode, sending too many messages, taking on too much—and eventually my wife has to tell me I’m going too far, or at work my director has to rein me in. That’s basically why I came in. It feels like I’m driving with the accelerator stuck down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unusually happy or upbeat when you're launching into these big plans?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. “Happy” is maybe not quite the word I’d use the whole time, but there are definitely weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m firing on all cylinders and everything is lining up and I can just keep pushing forward. It’s this unstoppable feeling, like I can see the whole map in my head and I’m finally moving fast enough to match it.\n\nBut it can turn really fast. Like if my wife asks a reasonable question—“Do you need to do this tonight?” or “Can this wait?”—or if there’s some tiny delay, it feels way bigger than it should. I’ll snap or get irritated, like they’re deliberately putting brakes on me, even though later I can see that’s probably not what’s happening. In the moment it feels like everyone is trying to slow me down on purpose, and I get this surge of frustration, like, “Why are you blocking me when I’m finally getting somewhere?”\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling electric and unstoppable happen at fairly regular intervals?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s like clockwork, no. It’s not like every three months or on some predictable schedule that I can point to. It’s more that I’ll have stretches—some weeks—where I feel almost electric and unstoppable, like everything feels urgent and possible and I’m moving faster than everyone else around me.\n\nAnd then, during those stretches, my mood can flip really quickly into irritation. Like a tiny delay, or my wife just asking me a reasonable question, can set me off. I’ll feel like she’s trying to slow me down on purpose, even if later I can tell that’s not really fair. In the moment it feels like people are blocking me when I’m finally getting traction. So… not regular intervals exactly, but these periods do seem to come in runs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just being tired or having an off morning. There are stretches where I wake up and it’s like before I even open my eyes fully, I already feel this weight on me. Like I’m a failed version of myself, if that makes sense. Not one specific failure, necessarily—more like my whole self feels… disappointing. Heavy and flat.\n\nAnd it can last most of the day. I’ll be sitting there knowing I need to get up, answer a message, respond to my wife asking something totally normal like, “Did you want coffee?” or “What time are you leaving?” and even that feels like too much to process. Not because she’s doing anything wrong. It’s just like there’s no room in me for anything, even basic conversation. I feel empty, kind of shut down, and I hate it because I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m being cold or difficult, but inside I’m just… flattened.\n\nDoctor: Do your stretches of feeling electric and unstoppable usually end with you feeling sad or empty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of the time it does feel like there’s a crash afterward. I don’t know if it’s always immediately after, like a clean pattern where one ends and the other begins, but I’ll go from that wired, driven feeling to these stretches where I wake up already feeling like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because nothing necessarily has to happen first. I’ll open my eyes and it’s just there—this heavy, flat feeling, like I’m a disappointing version of myself. And then even really normal things feel like too much. My wife might ask something simple, like what time I’m leaving or whether I want coffee, and I know it’s a normal question, but inside it feels like I don’t have enough space to answer. Like even forming a response is work.\n\nSo… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Does the sad or empty feeling usually start right after one of these electric periods ends?\n\nPatient: Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.\n\nAnd I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.\n\nSo I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these brief comments when no one is there often?\n\nPatient: Yeah… often enough that I notice it, I guess. Not constantly, like it isn’t a full conversation happening all day, but it happens more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nIt’s usually brief—like I’ll hear my name, just my name, in this way that makes me stop what I’m doing. Or it’ll be a short comment, almost like it’s tucked inside background noise. At work, especially around the server-room hum, I’ll think I hear something like, “check the logs,” and I’ll actually pause because that sounds like something relevant to my job, so part of me is like, okay, did someone say that or did I just… hear it in the noise?\n\nAt home it happens too, in my office when nobody else is there. Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. And then I’m sitting there trying to figure out if it came from outside, or the computer speakers, or if my wife said something from another room, but there isn’t anyone there. It’s not usually loud and dramatic. It’s more like a comment slipping through the hum of things. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these comments when you're feeling electric and unstoppable, when you're feeling sad or empty, or at any time regardless of your mood?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m electric, no. That would almost make more sense to me, honestly, like if it only happened when I was sped up and not sleeping much or feeling unstoppable. And it’s not only when I’m really low either.\n\nThere have been these stretches where I’m… I don’t know, calmer? Flat maybe. Not especially up, not in that awful heavy depression either. I’m still going to work, answering emails, doing the regular routine, so from the outside I probably seem more normal. But even then, sometimes for a few weeks at a time, I’ll still get that feeling that things are being signaled to me.\n\nLike I mentioned with Teams statuses—the little green, yellow, red indicators, or when someone changes availability—it can start feeling like coded warnings. And during those same calmer stretches, I’ll still hear the brief comments too, even when no one is there. Not all day, not like a constant voice, but enough that I stop and check.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… it can happen regardless of my mood. It may feel more intense when I’m already revved up or really down, but it doesn’t disappear just because I’m not in one of those states. That’s part of what scares me a little, because I can’t just explain it away as “oh, that was only when I was electric” or “only when I was depressed.”\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear when no one is there seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It seems like background hum is a big trigger, or maybe not a trigger exactly, but where it slips in the most. Like in the server room at work, with that constant fan noise and equipment noise, I’ll suddenly hear my name or something short, like “check the logs,” and it feels just clear enough that I stop and listen. Since that phrase actually relates to my job, it can take me a second to separate whether it was real or just… in the hum.\n\nAt home it can happen in my office too, especially if it’s quiet but there’s still some kind of computer noise or house noise. I’ll hear something like “they know,” and I’ll look around or pause whatever I’m doing, because it feels like it came from somewhere nearby even though nobody else is there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not usually out of total silence. It’s more like it gets shaped out of background noise, if that makes sense. The server-room hum is probably the clearest example.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these brief comments, are they usually telling you to do something or are they just phrases without any specific action?\n\nPatient: Usually they’re more like short phrases than full instructions. Like I’ll hear my name, or something like “they know,” and there isn’t really an action attached to that—it just makes me stop and feel like I’m supposed to understand something.\n\n“Check the logs” is the one that feels closest to telling me to do something, because that actually is an action, and it fits my job so well that I’ll sometimes pause and think, okay, should I check them? Did someone say that because there’s a problem? But it’s not like a whole voice giving me a list of commands or telling me step-by-step what to do. It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you always hear the brief comments, or are they sometimes more like thoughts that pop into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s a mix, I think, but the ones I’ve been trying to describe are not just thoughts, at least they don’t feel like my normal thoughts. Like, I know what it feels like when a thought pops into my head—like remembering I forgot to send an email or suddenly thinking about a meeting. That feels internal, like it’s in my own stream.\n\nThese are different. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? But in the moment, it lands more like I heard it. It’s brief, not a whole conversation, but it makes me stop what I’m doing and listen for a second.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these brief comments, do you ever see anything unusual or is it only something you hear?\n\nPatient: It’s only something I hear, as far as I can tell. I don’t see anything with it, like no shapes or people or anything moving around that shouldn’t be there.\n\nIt’s more that the sound in the room starts to feel like it has words in it. Like in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll hear my name or something short like “check the logs,” and I’ll stop because for a second it seems like someone nearby said it. Or at home in my office, when I’m alone, I might hear something like “they know.” But I’m not seeing anyone there when it happens. I’ll look around sometimes, just automatically, but there’s nothing visual that goes along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear happen more often when you're in one of your electric or sad periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they seem to happen more in the “electric” periods, if that’s what you mean by the driven ones. Like when I’m barely sleeping and I’m really keyed up, pushing these huge plans at work or in my head, the comments in the server noise get more noticeable.\n\nIt’s not just that I’m tired and mishearing things—although maybe that’s part of it—but during those stretches I’ll hear something like “check the logs” or my name in the equipment hum, and it feels more loaded, like it connects to whatever I’m working on. It’s not usually loud and dramatic. It’s more like a comment slipping through the hum of things. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these comments when you're feeling electric and unstoppable, when you're feeling sad or empty, or at any time regardless of your mood?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m electric, no. That would almost make more sense to me, honestly, like if it only happened when I was sped up and not sleeping much or feeling unstoppable. And it’s not only when I’m really low either.\n\nThere have been these stretches where I’m… I don’t know, calmer? Flat maybe. Not especially up, not in that awful heavy depression either. I’m still going to work, answering emails, doing the regular routine, so from the outside I probably seem more normal. But even then, sometimes for a few weeks at a time, I’ll still get that feeling that things are being signaled to me.\n\nLike I mentioned with Teams statuses—the little green, yellow, red indicators, or when someone changes availability—it can start feeling like coded warnings. And during those same calmer stretches, I’ll still hear the brief comments too, even when no one is there. Not all day, not like a constant voice, but enough that I stop and check.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… it can happen regardless of my mood. It may feel more intense when I’m already revved up or really down, but it doesn’t disappear just because I’m not in one of those states. That’s part of what scares me a little, because I can’t just explain it away as “oh, that was only when I was electric” or “only when I was depressed.”\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear when no one is there seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It seems like background hum is a big trigger, or maybe not a trigger exactly, but where it slips in the most. Like in the server room at work, with that constant fan noise and equipment noise, I’ll suddenly hear my name or something short, like “check the logs,” and it feels just clear enough that I stop and listen. Since that phrase actually relates to my job, it can take me a second to separate whether it was real or just… in the hum.\n\nAt home it can happen in my office too, especially if it’s quiet but there’s still some kind of computer noise or house noise. I’ll hear something like “they know,” and I’ll look around or pause whatever I’m doing, because it feels like it came from somewhere nearby even though nobody else is there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not usually out of total silence. It’s more like it gets shaped out of background noise, if that makes sense. The server-room hum is probably the clearest example.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these brief comments, are they usually telling you to do something or are they just phrases without any specific action?\n\nPatient: Usually they’re more like short phrases than full instructions. Like I’ll hear my name, or something like “they know,” and there isn’t really an action attached to that—it just makes me stop and feel like I’m supposed to understand something.\n\n“Check the logs” is the one that feels closest to telling me to do something, because that actually is an action, and it fits my job so well that I’ll sometimes pause and think, okay, should I check them? Did someone say that because there’s a problem? But it’s not like a whole voice giving me a list of commands or telling me step-by-step what to do. It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you always hear the brief comments, or are they sometimes more like thoughts that pop into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s a mix, I think, but the ones I’ve been trying to describe are not just thoughts, at least they don’t feel like my normal thoughts. Like, I know what it feels like when a thought pops into my head—like remembering I forgot to send an email or suddenly thinking about a meeting. That feels internal, like it’s in my own stream.\n\nThese are different. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? But in the moment, it lands more like I heard it. It’s brief, not a whole conversation, but it makes me stop what I’m doing and listen for a second.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these brief comments, do you ever see anything unusual or is it only something you hear?\n\nPatient: It’s only something I hear, as far as I can tell. I don’t see anything with it, like no shapes or people or anything moving around that shouldn’t be there.\n\nIt’s more that the sound in the room starts to feel like it has words in it. Like in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll hear my name or something short like “check the logs,” and I’ll stop because for a second it seems like someone nearby said it. Or at home in my office, when I’m alone, I might hear something like “they know.” But I’m not seeing anyone there when it happens. I’ll look around sometimes, just automatically, but there’s nothing visual that goes along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear happen more often when you're in one of your electric or sad periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they seem to happen more in the “electric” periods, if that’s what you mean by the driven ones. Like when I’m barely sleeping and I’m really keyed up, pushing these huge plans at work or in my head, the comments in the server noise get more noticeable.\n\nIt’s not just that I’m tired and mishearing things—although maybe that’s part of it—but during those stretches I’ll hear something like “check the logs” or my name in the equipment hum, and it feels more loaded, like it connects to whatever I’m working on. I also start reading more into work messages then, like emails or chat messages feel like they have hidden threats or warnings in them, even if later I can sometimes see maybe they were just normal messages.\n\nWhen I’m sad, I don’t know… I’m more shut down, and I’m not usually pushing all those big plans. I might still be anxious or stuck in my head, but the brief comments seem more tied to those driven, barely-sleeping periods than the sad ones.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

In the moment, it feels like it’s coming from outside me. That’s the unsettling part. If it were just a thought, I think I’d recognize it as my own internal voice, but this is more like I’m hearing it in the room — like it’s mixed into the server-room hum or the fan noise at home.

Then after a few seconds, when there’s nobody there, I can kind of question it and think, okay, maybe my brain is pulling words out of the background noise. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I decided to think those words. It feels like they arrived from outside my head.

Like a tiny delay, or my wife just asking me a reasonable question, can set me off. I’ll feel like she’s trying to slow me down on purpose, even if later I can tell that’s not really fair. I’ll be sitting there knowing I need to get up, answer a message, respond to my wife asking something totally normal like, “Did you want coffee?” or “What time are you leaving?” and even that feels like too much to process. Not because she’s doing anything wrong. It’s just like there’s no room in me for anything, even basic conversation. I feel empty, kind of shut down, and I hate it because I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m being cold or difficult, but inside I’m just… flattened.\n\nDoctor: Do your stretches of feeling electric and unstoppable usually end with you feeling sad or empty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of the time it does feel like there’s a crash afterward. I don’t know if it’s always immediately after, like a clean pattern where one ends and the other begins, but I’ll go from that wired, driven feeling to these stretches where I wake up already feeling like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because nothing necessarily has to happen first. I’ll open my eyes and it’s just there—this heavy, flat feeling, like I’m a disappointing version of myself. And then even really normal things feel like too much. My wife might ask something simple, like what time I’m leaving or whether I want coffee, and I know it’s a normal question, but inside it feels like I don’t have enough space to answer. Like even forming a response is work.\n\nSo… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Does the sad or empty feeling usually start right after one of these electric periods ends?\n\nPatient: Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.\n\nAnd I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.\n\nSo I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these brief comments when no one is there often?\n\nPatient: Yeah… often enough that I notice it, I guess. Not constantly, like it isn’t a full conversation happening all day, but it happens more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nIt’s usually brief—like I’ll hear my name, just my name, in this way that makes me stop what I’m doing. Or it’ll be a short comment, almost like it’s tucked inside background noise. At work, especially around the server-room hum, I’ll think I hear something like, “check the logs,” and I’ll actually pause because that sounds like something relevant to my job, so part of me is like, okay, did someone say that or did I just… hear it in the noise?\n\nAt home it happens too, in my office when nobody else is there. Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. And then I’m sitting there trying to figure out if it came from outside, or the computer speakers, or if my wife said something from another room, but there isn’t anyone there. It’s not usually loud and dramatic. It’s more like a comment slipping through the hum of things. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these comments when you're feeling electric and unstoppable, when you're feeling sad or empty, or at any time regardless of your mood?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m electric, no. That would almost make more sense to me, honestly, like if it only happened when I was sped up and not sleeping much or feeling unstoppable. And it’s not only when I’m really low either.\n\nThere have been these stretches where I’m… I don’t know, calmer? Flat maybe. Not especially up, not in that awful heavy depression either. I’m still going to work, answering emails, doing the regular routine, so from the outside I probably seem more normal. But even then, sometimes for a few weeks at a time, I’ll still get that feeling that things are being signaled to me.\n\nLike I mentioned with Teams statuses—the little green, yellow, red indicators, or when someone changes availability—it can start feeling like coded warnings. And during those same calmer stretches, I’ll still hear the brief comments too, even when no one is there. Not all day, not like a constant voice, but enough that I stop and check.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… it can happen regardless of my mood. It may feel more intense when I’m already revved up or really down, but it doesn’t disappear just because I’m not in one of those states. That’s part of what scares me a little, because I can’t just explain it away as “oh, that was only when I was electric” or “only when I was depressed.”\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear when no one is there seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It seems like background hum is a big trigger, or maybe not a trigger exactly, but where it slips in the most. Like in the server room at work, with that constant fan noise and equipment noise, I’ll suddenly hear my name or something short, like “check the logs,” and it feels just clear enough that I stop and listen. Since that phrase actually relates to my job, it can take me a second to separate whether it was real or just… in the hum.\n\nAt home it can happen in my office too, especially if it’s quiet but there’s still some kind of computer noise or house noise. I’ll hear something like “they know,” and I’ll look around or pause whatever I’m doing, because it feels like it came from somewhere nearby even though nobody else is there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not usually out of total silence. It’s more like it gets shaped out of background noise, if that makes sense. The server-room hum is probably the clearest example.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these brief comments, are they usually telling you to do something or are they just phrases without any specific action?\n\nPatient: Usually they’re more like short phrases than full instructions. Like I’ll hear my name, or something like “they know,” and there isn’t really an action attached to that—it just makes me stop and feel like I’m supposed to understand something.\n\n“Check the logs” is the one that feels closest to telling me to do something, because that actually is an action, and it fits my job so well that I’ll sometimes pause and think, okay, should I check them? Did someone say that because there’s a problem? But it’s not like a whole voice giving me a list of commands or telling me step-by-step what to do. It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you always hear the brief comments, or are they sometimes more like thoughts that pop into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s a mix, I think, but the ones I’ve been trying to describe are not just thoughts, at least they don’t feel like my normal thoughts. Like, I know what it feels like when a thought pops into my head—like remembering I forgot to send an email or suddenly thinking about a meeting. That feels internal, like it’s in my own stream.\n\nThese are different. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? But in the moment, it lands more like I heard it. It’s brief, not a whole conversation, but it makes me stop what I’m doing and listen for a second.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these brief comments, do you ever see anything unusual or is it only something you hear?\n\nPatient: It’s only something I hear, as far as I can tell. I don’t see anything with it, like no shapes or people or anything moving around that shouldn’t be there.\n\nIt’s more that the sound in the room starts to feel like it has words in it. Like in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll hear my name or something short like “check the logs,” and I’ll stop because for a second it seems like someone nearby said it. Or at home in my office, when I’m alone, I might hear something like “they know.” But I’m not seeing anyone there when it happens. I’ll look around sometimes, just automatically, but there’s nothing visual that goes along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear happen more often when you're in one of your electric or sad periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they seem to happen more in the “electric” periods, if that’s what you mean by the driven ones. Like when I’m barely sleeping and I’m really keyed up, pushing these huge plans at work or in my head, the comments in the server noise get more noticeable.\n\nIt’s not just that I’m tired and mishearing things—although maybe that’s part of it—but during those stretches I’ll hear something like “check the logs” or my name in the equipment hum, and it feels more loaded, like it connects to whatever I’m working on. I also start reading more into work messages then, like emails or chat messages feel like they have hidden threats or warnings in them, even if later I can sometimes see maybe they were just normal messages.\n\nWhen I’m sad, I don’t know… I’m more shut down, and I’m not usually pushing all those big plans. I might still be anxious or stuck in my head, but the brief comments seem more tied to those driven, barely-sleeping periods than the sad ones.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the brief comments you hear are actually coming from outside your mind or are they just a product of your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels like it’s coming from outside me. That’s the unsettling part. If it were just a thought, I think I’d recognize it as my own internal voice, but this is more like I’m hearing it in the room — like it’s mixed into the server-room hum or the fan noise at home.\n\nLike I’ll hear my name, or something short like “check the logs,” or “they know,” and I’ll actually stop and look up, because it feels like someone said it nearby. Then after a few seconds, when there’s nobody there, I can kind of question it and think, okay, maybe my brain is pulling words out of the background noise. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I decided to think those words. It feels like they arrived from outside my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type). Yeah… they can. I mean, it’s definitely more obvious when I’m in the electric periods, but it’s not only then.

There have been stretches where I’d say I was pretty neutral — not especially up, not crashing or sad, just kind of calm or flat, going through my normal routine. And even during those times, I’ve still had some of the same stuff happen. Like for a few weeks at a time, I wasn’t sleeping great maybe, but I wasn’t in that driven, big-plan mode, and I still would hear a short comment when no one was there. Not constant, but enough that I noticed it.

And the Teams thing happened during those calmer stretches too. I’d see someone’s status change or an away message or whatever, and it would feel like it was a coded warning meant for me. Like not just “oh, that’s odd,” but I’d actually believe there was some meaning in it. Then later I’d try to talk myself down, but at the time it felt real.

So, yes — it’s more tied to the electric times, but it has happened when I felt basically neutral too.

Like I’ll hear my name, or something like “they know,” and there isn’t really an action attached to that—it just makes me stop and feel like I’m supposed to understand something.\n\n“Check the logs” is the one that feels closest to telling me to do something, because that actually is an action, and it fits my job so well that I’ll sometimes pause and think, okay, should I check them? Did someone say that because there’s a problem? But it’s not like a whole voice giving me a list of commands or telling me step-by-step what to do. It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you always hear the brief comments, or are they sometimes more like thoughts that pop into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s a mix, I think, but the ones I’ve been trying to describe are not just thoughts, at least they don’t feel like my normal thoughts. Like, I know what it feels like when a thought pops into my head—like remembering I forgot to send an email or suddenly thinking about a meeting. That feels internal, like it’s in my own stream.\n\nThese are different. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? But in the moment, it lands more like I heard it. It’s brief, not a whole conversation, but it makes me stop what I’m doing and listen for a second.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these brief comments, do you ever see anything unusual or is it only something you hear?\n\nPatient: It’s only something I hear, as far as I can tell. I don’t see anything with it, like no shapes or people or anything moving around that shouldn’t be there.\n\nIt’s more that the sound in the room starts to feel like it has words in it. Like in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll hear my name or something short like “check the logs,” and I’ll stop because for a second it seems like someone nearby said it. Or at home in my office, when I’m alone, I might hear something like “they know.” But I’m not seeing anyone there when it happens. I’ll look around sometimes, just automatically, but there’s nothing visual that goes along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear happen more often when you're in one of your electric or sad periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they seem to happen more in the “electric” periods, if that’s what you mean by the driven ones. Like when I’m barely sleeping and I’m really keyed up, pushing these huge plans at work or in my head, the comments in the server noise get more noticeable.\n\nIt’s not just that I’m tired and mishearing things—although maybe that’s part of it—but during those stretches I’ll hear something like “check the logs” or my name in the equipment hum, and it feels more loaded, like it connects to whatever I’m working on. I also start reading more into work messages then, like emails or chat messages feel like they have hidden threats or warnings in them, even if later I can sometimes see maybe they were just normal messages.\n\nWhen I’m sad, I don’t know… I’m more shut down, and I’m not usually pushing all those big plans. I might still be anxious or stuck in my head, but the brief comments seem more tied to those driven, barely-sleeping periods than the sad ones.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the brief comments you hear are actually coming from outside your mind or are they just a product of your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels like it’s coming from outside me. That’s the unsettling part. If it were just a thought, I think I’d recognize it as my own internal voice, but this is more like I’m hearing it in the room — like it’s mixed into the server-room hum or the fan noise at home.\n\nLike I’ll hear my name, or something short like “check the logs,” or “they know,” and I’ll actually stop and look up, because it feels like someone said it nearby. Then after a few seconds, when there’s nobody there, I can kind of question it and think, okay, maybe my brain is pulling words out of the background noise. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I decided to think those words. It feels like they arrived from outside my head.\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear ever happen when you're feeling completely neutral, not electric or sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they can. I mean, it’s definitely more obvious when I’m in the electric periods, but it’s not only then.\n\nThere have been stretches where I’d say I was pretty neutral — not especially up, not crashing or sad, just kind of calm or flat, going through my normal routine. And even during those times, I’ve still had some of the same stuff happen. Like for a few weeks at a time, I wasn’t sleeping great maybe, but I wasn’t in that driven, big-plan mode, and I still would hear a short comment when no one was there. Not constant, but enough that I noticed it.\n\nAnd the Teams thing happened during those calmer stretches too. I’d see someone’s status change or an away message or whatever, and it would feel like it was a coded warning meant for me. Like not just “oh, that’s odd,” but I’d actually believe there was some meaning in it. Then later I’d try to talk myself down, but at the time it felt real.\n\nSo, yes — it’s more tied to the electric times, but it has happened when I felt basically neutral too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… I’ve wondered that. Especially because it’s not usually like a clear person standing behind me talking in a normal voice. It’s more like the words sort of come out of the noise, if that makes sense.

In the server room, there’s this constant hum — fans, equipment, airflow, all of that — and sometimes in that I’ll hear my name, or a short thing like “check the logs,” or “they know.” And at home in my office, it can happen with background noise too, like the fan or computer noise, when nobody else is there. So part of me can say, okay, maybe my brain is grabbing onto patterns in the sound and turning them into words.

But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels like I actually heard it. I’ll stop what I’m doing and listen, because it feels external in that moment. Then afterward I can question it more. So yes, it could be misinterpreting the hum or background noise, but in the moment it feels a lot more real than that.

Then later I’d try to talk myself down, but at the time it felt real.\n\nSo, yes — it’s more tied to the electric times, but it has happened when I felt basically neutral too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the brief comments could be your brain misinterpreting the sounds around you, like the server room hum or other background noises?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’ve wondered that. Especially because it’s not usually like a clear person standing behind me talking in a normal voice. It’s more like the words sort of come out of the noise, if that makes sense.\n\nIn the server room, there’s this constant hum — fans, equipment, airflow, all of that — and sometimes in that I’ll hear my name, or a short thing like “check the logs,” or “they know.” And at home in my office, it can happen with background noise too, like the fan or computer noise, when nobody else is there. So part of me can say, okay, maybe my brain is grabbing onto patterns in the sound and turning them into words.\n\nBut when it happens, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels like I actually heard it. I’ll stop what I’m doing and listen, because it feels external in that moment. Then afterward I can question it more. So yes, it could be misinterpreting the hum or background noise, but in the moment it feels a lot more real than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think that’s probably part of it. I mean, the pattern is pretty hard to ignore because it’s usually in those situations where there’s already some kind of steady noise going on.

Like in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll suddenly hear my name, or a short phrase — “check the logs,” or “they know” — and it’s not like there’s an actual person there saying it. Same thing can happen in my home office when I’m alone and there’s just the computer noise or a fan running. So, rationally, I can see how my brain might be grabbing onto background sound and turning it into words.

But it’s still strange because in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m mishearing a fan.” It feels like I heard a comment. I’ll pause, listen harder, sometimes look around, and only afterward do I think, okay, maybe that was just the hum getting interpreted wrong.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because things have kind of gotten out of hand with how much I’m trying to do. It’s not just being busy. I mean, I have a demanding job already—I manage computer systems and projects, so I’m used to juggling a lot—but lately I keep launching into these big plans one after another, like work initiatives, certification schedules, redesigning my home network, reaching out to old contacts with long messages and ideas… and I can’t seem to stop once I get going.\n\nIt starts feeling important, like if I don’t act on it right now I’m going to lose the momentum or miss some opportunity. Then I blow past normal limits—staying in planning mode, sending too many messages, taking on too much—and eventually my wife has to tell me I’m going too far, or at work my director has to rein me in. That’s basically why I came in. It feels like I’m driving with the accelerator stuck down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unusually happy or upbeat when you're launching into these big plans?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. “Happy” is maybe not quite the word I’d use the whole time, but there are definitely weeks where I feel almost electric, like I’m firing on all cylinders and everything is lining up and I can just keep pushing forward. It’s this unstoppable feeling, like I can see the whole map in my head and I’m finally moving fast enough to match it.\n\nBut it can turn really fast. Like if my wife asks a reasonable question—“Do you need to do this tonight?” or “Can this wait?”—or if there’s some tiny delay, it feels way bigger than it should. I’ll snap or get irritated, like they’re deliberately putting brakes on me, even though later I can see that’s probably not what’s happening. In the moment it feels like everyone is trying to slow me down on purpose, and I get this surge of frustration, like, “Why are you blocking me when I’m finally getting somewhere?”\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling electric and unstoppable happen at fairly regular intervals?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s like clockwork, no. It’s not like every three months or on some predictable schedule that I can point to. It’s more that I’ll have stretches—some weeks—where I feel almost electric and unstoppable, like everything feels urgent and possible and I’m moving faster than everyone else around me.\n\nAnd then, during those stretches, my mood can flip really quickly into irritation. Like a tiny delay, or my wife just asking me a reasonable question, can set me off. I’ll feel like she’s trying to slow me down on purpose, even if later I can tell that’s not really fair. In the moment it feels like people are blocking me when I’m finally getting traction. So… not regular intervals exactly, but these periods do seem to come in runs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just being tired or having an off morning. There are stretches where I wake up and it’s like before I even open my eyes fully, I already feel this weight on me. Like I’m a failed version of myself, if that makes sense. Not one specific failure, necessarily—more like my whole self feels… disappointing. Heavy and flat.\n\nAnd it can last most of the day. I’ll be sitting there knowing I need to get up, answer a message, respond to my wife asking something totally normal like, “Did you want coffee?” or “What time are you leaving?” and even that feels like too much to process. I feel empty, kind of shut down, and I hate it because I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m being cold or difficult, but inside I’m just… flattened.\n\nDoctor: Do your stretches of feeling electric and unstoppable usually end with you feeling sad or empty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of the time it does feel like there’s a crash afterward. I don’t know if it’s always immediately after, like a clean pattern where one ends and the other begins, but I’ll go from that wired, driven feeling to these stretches where I wake up already feeling like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because nothing necessarily has to happen first. I’ll open my eyes and it’s just there—this heavy, flat feeling, like I’m a disappointing version of myself. And then even really normal things feel like too much. My wife might ask something simple, like what time I’m leaving or whether I want coffee, and I know it’s a normal question, but inside it feels like I don’t have enough space to answer. Like even forming a response is work.\n\nSo… yes, I guess the “electric” periods can end with me feeling sad or empty, but it’s less like a dramatic ending and more like I wake up and the whole world has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Does the sad or empty feeling usually start right after one of these electric periods ends?\n\nPatient: Not always right after, no. That’s the part I have trouble pinning down. Sometimes it feels like there’s a crash, like I’ve been running on this intense current and then suddenly I wake up and everything is just… gone flat. But I can’t honestly say it’s always the next day or the same evening or anything that neat.\n\nWhat I notice more is the way the sad part starts when it does start. I’ll wake up already feeling like I’m some failed version of myself, before anything has even happened. It’s this heavy, flat feeling, like there’s no momentum left in me at all. And then even normal things feel too big. My wife can ask something completely ordinary—like whether I want coffee, or what time I’m leaving—and I know it’s normal, I know she’s not doing anything wrong, but answering feels like too much. Like I don’t have enough space in my head to even form the response.\n\nSo… sometimes it seems connected to the end of those electric periods, but I don’t know if it’s always directly right after. The depressed feeling just kind of settles in, and once it’s there, it takes over most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're back to your normal self between these electric periods?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. That’s kind of what makes it confusing for me, because there are definitely stretches where I’m not “electric,” like I’m not racing around feeling unstoppable, and I’m also not in that really heavy depressed place either. On the surface those periods can look calmer, maybe even normal-ish.\n\nBut even during those calmer or flatter stretches, I don’t always feel fully back to myself. There have been times—sometimes a few weeks at a time—where I’m functioning, going through work stuff, doing regular things, and I’m not especially up or down… but I still have these thoughts that something is being signaled to me. Like Teams statuses at work, the little availability colors or changes, start feeling like coded warnings. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like there’s meaning there, like people are indirectly telling me something or warning me.\n\nAnd I’ll still sometimes hear brief comments when no one is there. Not long conversations, just quick little remarks, enough that I stop and kind of check around or replay what just happened.\n\nSo I guess if “normal self” means not manic and not depressed, then sometimes, sort of. But if it means my mind feels clear and I’m not reading warnings into things or hearing stuff, then no—not always. There are calm stretches where those things are still going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these brief comments when no one is there often?\n\nPatient: Yeah… often enough that I notice it, I guess. Not constantly, like it isn’t a full conversation happening all day, but it happens more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nIt’s usually brief—like I’ll hear my name, just my name, in this way that makes me stop what I’m doing. Or it’ll be a short comment, almost like it’s tucked inside background noise. At work, especially around the server-room hum, I’ll think I hear something like, “check the logs,” and I’ll actually pause because that sounds like something relevant to my job, so part of me is like, okay, did someone say that or did I just… hear it in the noise?\n\nAt home it happens too, in my office when nobody else is there. Sometimes it’s just a quick phrase, like “they know,” and then it’s gone. And then I’m sitting there trying to figure out if it came from outside, or the computer speakers, or if my wife said something from another room, but there isn’t anyone there. It’s not usually loud and dramatic. It’s more like a comment slipping through the hum of things. But yeah, it happens often enough that I’ll check, and I don’t always like what it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these comments when you're feeling electric and unstoppable, when you're feeling sad or empty, or at any time regardless of your mood?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m electric, no. That would almost make more sense to me, honestly, like if it only happened when I was sped up and not sleeping much or feeling unstoppable. And it’s not only when I’m really low either.\n\nThere have been these stretches where I’m… I don’t know, calmer? Flat maybe. Not especially up, not in that awful heavy depression either. I’m still going to work, answering emails, doing the regular routine, so from the outside I probably seem more normal. But even then, sometimes for a few weeks at a time, I’ll still get that feeling that things are being signaled to me.\n\nLike I mentioned with Teams statuses—the little green, yellow, red indicators, or when someone changes availability—it can start feeling like coded warnings. And during those same calmer stretches, I’ll still hear the brief comments too, even when no one is there. Not all day, not like a constant voice, but enough that I stop and check.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… it can happen regardless of my mood. It may feel more intense when I’m already revved up or really down, but it doesn’t disappear just because I’m not in one of those states. That’s part of what scares me a little, because I can’t just explain it away as “oh, that was only when I was electric” or “only when I was depressed.”\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear when no one is there seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It seems like background hum is a big trigger, or maybe not a trigger exactly, but where it slips in the most. Like in the server room at work, with that constant fan noise and equipment noise, I’ll suddenly hear my name or something short, like “check the logs,” and it feels just clear enough that I stop and listen. Since that phrase actually relates to my job, it can take me a second to separate whether it was real or just… in the hum.\n\nAt home it can happen in my office too, especially if it’s quiet but there’s still some kind of computer noise or house noise. I’ll hear something like “they know,” and I’ll look around or pause whatever I’m doing, because it feels like it came from somewhere nearby even though nobody else is there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not usually out of total silence. It’s more like it gets shaped out of background noise, if that makes sense. The server-room hum is probably the clearest example.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these brief comments, are they usually telling you to do something or are they just phrases without any specific action?\n\nPatient: Usually they’re more like short phrases than full instructions. Like I’ll hear my name, or something like “they know,” and there isn’t really an action attached to that—it just makes me stop and feel like I’m supposed to understand something.\n\n“Check the logs” is the one that feels closest to telling me to do something, because that actually is an action, and it fits my job so well that I’ll sometimes pause and think, okay, should I check them? Did someone say that because there’s a problem? But it’s not like a whole voice giving me a list of commands or telling me step-by-step what to do. It’s more like these brief comments slipping out of the server-room hum, or sometimes from my home office when nobody else is there, and then I’m left trying to decide if it meant anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you always hear the brief comments, or are they sometimes more like thoughts that pop into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s a mix, I think, but the ones I’ve been trying to describe are not just thoughts, at least they don’t feel like my normal thoughts. Like, I know what it feels like when a thought pops into my head—like remembering I forgot to send an email or suddenly thinking about a meeting. That feels internal, like it’s in my own stream.\n\nThese are different. I’ll be in the server room with the fan noise and equipment hum, or at home in my office when there’s nobody around, and I’ll hear my name or a short comment like “check the logs” or “they know.” It feels like it’s coming from the room, or from the noise somehow, not like I sat there and thought the words on purpose.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll question it and think, was that just my brain making a phrase out of the hum? But in the moment, it lands more like I heard it. It’s brief, not a whole conversation, but it makes me stop what I’m doing and listen for a second.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these brief comments, do you ever see anything unusual or is it only something you hear?\n\nPatient: It’s only something I hear, as far as I can tell. I don’t see anything with it, like no shapes or people or anything moving around that shouldn’t be there.\n\nIt’s more that the sound in the room starts to feel like it has words in it. Like in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll hear my name or something short like “check the logs,” and I’ll stop because for a second it seems like someone nearby said it. Or at home in my office, when I’m alone, I might hear something like “they know.” But I’m not seeing anyone there when it happens. I’ll look around sometimes, just automatically, but there’s nothing visual that goes along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear happen more often when you're in one of your electric or sad periods?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they seem to happen more in the “electric” periods, if that’s what you mean by the driven ones. Like when I’m barely sleeping and I’m really keyed up, pushing these huge plans at work or in my head, the comments in the server noise get more noticeable.\n\nIt’s not just that I’m tired and mishearing things—although maybe that’s part of it—but during those stretches I’ll hear something like “check the logs” or my name in the equipment hum, and it feels more loaded, like it connects to whatever I’m working on. I also start reading more into work messages then, like emails or chat messages feel like they have hidden threats or warnings in them, even if later I can sometimes see maybe they were just normal messages.\n\nWhen I’m sad, I don’t know… I’m more shut down, and I’m not usually pushing all those big plans. I might still be anxious or stuck in my head, but the brief comments seem more tied to those driven, barely-sleeping periods than the sad ones.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the brief comments you hear are actually coming from outside your mind or are they just a product of your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels like it’s coming from outside me. That’s the unsettling part. If it were just a thought, I think I’d recognize it as my own internal voice, but this is more like I’m hearing it in the room — like it’s mixed into the server-room hum or the fan noise at home.\n\nLike I’ll hear my name, or something short like “check the logs,” or “they know,” and I’ll actually stop and look up, because it feels like someone said it nearby. Then after a few seconds, when there’s nobody there, I can kind of question it and think, okay, maybe my brain is pulling words out of the background noise. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I decided to think those words. It feels like they arrived from outside my head.\n\nDoctor: Do the brief comments you hear ever happen when you're feeling completely neutral, not electric or sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they can. I mean, it’s definitely more obvious when I’m in the electric periods, but it’s not only then.\n\nThere have been stretches where I’d say I was pretty neutral — not especially up, not crashing or sad, just kind of calm or flat, going through my normal routine. And even during those times, I’ve still had some of the same stuff happen. Like for a few weeks at a time, I wasn’t sleeping great maybe, but I wasn’t in that driven, big-plan mode, and I still would hear a short comment when no one was there. Not constant, but enough that I noticed it.\n\nAnd the Teams thing happened during those calmer stretches too. I’d see someone’s status change or an away message or whatever, and it would feel like it was a coded warning meant for me. Like not just “oh, that’s odd,” but I’d actually believe there was some meaning in it. Then later I’d try to talk myself down, but at the time it felt real.\n\nSo, yes — it’s more tied to the electric times, but it has happened when I felt basically neutral too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the brief comments could be your brain misinterpreting the sounds around you, like the server room hum or other background noises?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’ve wondered that. Especially because it’s not usually like a clear person standing behind me talking in a normal voice. It’s more like the words sort of come out of the noise, if that makes sense.\n\nIn the server room, there’s this constant hum — fans, equipment, airflow, all of that — and sometimes in that I’ll hear my name, or a short thing like “check the logs,” or “they know.” And at home in my office, it can happen with background noise too, like the fan or computer noise, when nobody else is there. So part of me can say, okay, maybe my brain is grabbing onto patterns in the sound and turning them into words.\n\nBut when it happens, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels like I actually heard it. I’ll stop what I’m doing and listen, because it feels external in that moment. Then afterward I can question it more. So yes, it could be misinterpreting the hum or background noise, but in the moment it feels a lot more real than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brain might be mistakenly turning background noises into these brief comments you hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably part of it. I mean, the pattern is pretty hard to ignore because it’s usually in those situations where there’s already some kind of steady noise going on.\n\nLike in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll suddenly hear my name, or a short phrase — “check the logs,” or “they know” — and it’s not like there’s an actual person there saying it. Same thing can happen in my home office when I’m alone and there’s just the computer noise or a fan running. So, rationally, I can see how my brain might be grabbing onto background sound and turning it into words.\n\nBut it’s still strange because in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m mishearing a fan.” It feels like I heard a comment. I’ll pause, listen harder, sometimes look around, and only afterward do I think, okay, maybe that was just the hum getting interpreted wrong.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think stress or anxiety probably makes it more likely, or at least makes me notice it more and react to it more.

Like, if I’m already tense in the server room — say something’s down, or I’m worried I missed something — the hum of the fans and equipment almost feels like it has more “edges” to it, if that makes sense. That’s when I’ll hear my name, or a little comment like “check the logs,” and I’ll stop because it feels directed at me. Or something like “they know,” which is worse, because then I start trying to figure out what that means.

At home too, in my office, if I’m anxious and there’s the computer noise or a fan going, it can happen there when nobody else is around. It’s still brief, not like a whole conversation, but it lands like a real comment in the moment.

When I’m calm, I can sometimes step back faster and think, okay, maybe that was just the background noise. But when I’m stressed, it feels more convincing, and I get pulled into it more.

I mean, the pattern is pretty hard to ignore because it’s usually in those situations where there’s already some kind of steady noise going on.\n\nLike in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll suddenly hear my name, or a short phrase — “check the logs,” or “they know” — and it’s not like there’s an actual person there saying it. Same thing can happen in my home office when I’m alone and there’s just the computer noise or a fan running. So, rationally, I can see how my brain might be grabbing onto background sound and turning it into words.\n\nBut it’s still strange because in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m mishearing a fan.” It feels like I heard a comment. I’ll pause, listen harder, sometimes look around, and only afterward do I think, okay, maybe that was just the hum getting interpreted wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brain is mistakenly turning background noises into these brief comments you hear more often when you are stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think stress or anxiety probably makes it more likely, or at least makes me notice it more and react to it more.\n\nLike, if I’m already tense in the server room — say something’s down, or I’m worried I missed something — the hum of the fans and equipment almost feels like it has more “edges” to it, if that makes sense. It’s still brief, not like a whole conversation, but it lands like a real comment in the moment.\n\nWhen I’m calm, I can sometimes step back faster and think, okay, maybe that was just the background noise. I’d say… not completely tied to mood. Stress definitely seems to make it louder, or more convincing, like I was saying, but it’s not only when I’m anxious or depressed or really up.

There have been stretches where I felt pretty flat, honestly — not especially high, not especially low, just kind of going through the day — and it still happened. Sometimes for a few weeks at a time. During those periods I’d still catch myself thinking certain Teams statuses were like coded warnings, like someone was signaling something indirectly. And I’d still hear those brief comments when nobody was there, just little things that seemed to come out of the room noise or the computer noise.

So mood affects how much it grabs me, maybe, or how much I spiral around it. But the experiences themselves don’t seem to fully depend on my mood. They can show up even when I’d describe myself as pretty calm or emotionally neutral.

I mean, the pattern is pretty hard to ignore because it’s usually in those situations where there’s already some kind of steady noise going on.\n\nLike in the server room, with all the fans and equipment humming, I’ll suddenly hear my name, or a short phrase — “check the logs,” or “they know” — and it’s not like there’s an actual person there saying it.
